ENTRY 1  ROLE: user
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
<user_query>
ROBOT ARM CONTROL SYSTEM DESIGN (BEST PRACTICE)

Goal: Achieve smooth, CNC-quality motion for a multi-axis stepper robot arm using a laptop for simulation and a Raspberry Pi Pico 2 W for real-time control with PIO-driven stepper output.

SYSTEM OVERVIEW

The system is split into three layers:

Laptop (high-level planner)

Pico 2 W (real-time motion controller)

PIO + stepper drivers (hardware execution layer)

The key principle is that the laptop never directly controls motors in real time. It only sends motion intentions.

LAPTOP ROLE (NON REAL-TIME PLANNER)

The laptop runs simulation, inverse kinematics, and path planning. It generates joint trajectories and sends them as time-stamped joint positions or smooth trajectory points.

The laptop should:

Send joint angles at a steady update rate (50 to 200 Hz is ideal)

Include timestamps or consistent sampling intervals

Never send stepper pulses or direct motor commands

Treat the robot as a virtual model being tracked

Example behavior: send a continuous stream of joint positions over time, representing the desired motion path.

PICO ROLE (REAL-TIME MOTION CONTROLLER)

The Pico 2 W receives joint position data and does not execute it immediately. Instead, it maintains a rolling buffer of future motion, typically 20 to 50 milliseconds ahead.

The Pico is responsible for:

Receiving joint position streams

Storing a short future buffer of motion data

Interpolating between received points to fill gaps

Generating smooth motion profiles from that data

Computing velocity and acceleration per joint

Applying S-curve or jerk-limited smoothing for natural motion

The Pico acts as a motion “transformation layer” that converts imperfect incoming data into smooth, physically correct trajectories.

PIO ROLE (HARD REAL-TIME STEP GENERATION)

PIO is responsible only for generating step pulses with precise timing. It operates independently of CPU load and timing jitter.

PIO:

Runs dedicated state machines per motor axis

Generates STEP pulses at exact intervals

Does not perform motion planning or math

Executes timing values provided by the CPU

PIO ensures that once motion timing is computed, execution is perfectly stable and deterministic.

STEPPER DRIVERS (POWER STAGE)

Stepper drivers such as TMC2209 convert STEP and DIR signals into motor current.

They handle:

Microstepping for smoother motion

Current control

Motor driving efficiency

They do not handle motion logic or timing.

MOTION FLOW PIPELINE

The full system flow is:

Laptop generates joint trajectory

↓

Data sent over USB or Wi-Fi (non real-time)

↓

Pico receives and stores data in a rolling buffer (20 to 50 ms ahead)

↓

Pico interpolates between points to create smooth continuous motion

↓

Pico applies acceleration limiting and S-curve smoothing

↓

PIO receives step timing data

↓

PIO outputs perfectly timed step pulses

↓

Stepper drivers move motors

↓

Robot arm executes smooth motion

MOTION SMOOTHING STRATEGY

Smooth motion is achieved through three layers:

Buffering

The Pico always stays slightly ahead of real motion (20–50 ms buffer). This prevents communication delays from affecting motion.

Interpolation

Incoming joint positions are not used directly. The Pico generates intermediate values between points to create continuous motion.

S-curve acceleration

Acceleration is not instant. It is gradually ramped up and down to prevent vibration and mechanical stress.

CRITICAL DESIGN RULES

The laptop is not real-time and must never directly control motors

The Pico is responsible for transforming motion into real-time control

PIO is only responsible for precise timing, not motion logic

All motion must be buffered and interpolated before execution

Step generation must always be hardware-timed (PIO), never software-timed loops

WHY THIS DESIGN WORKS

This architecture works because it separates responsibilities correctly:

Laptop handles planning and simulation

Pico handles real-time correction and motion shaping

PIO guarantees perfect timing regardless of CPU load

This eliminates jitter, reduces vibration, and produces smooth robotic motion similar to CNC machines or industrial robot arms.

FINAL RESULT

With this architecture, the robot arm will:

Follow smooth trajectories instead of jerky motion

Remain stable even with network delays

Scale well to multiple joints

Achieve CNC-like precision and smoothness
</user_query>

ENTRY 5  ROLE: user

<user_query>
/explain do we currently use PIO
</user_query>

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
ENTRY 6  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
**No — the project does not use PIO today.**

Motion on the Pico is entirely **CPU-driven** in MicroPython:

- **`StepperAxis28BYJ`** — Updates four GPIO outputs each step from the `_HALFSTEP` table in `_step()`; timing comes from the **main loop** (`STEP_US`) and doing “one step” per planned burst, not from a PIO program.

- **`StepperAxisStepDir`** — Sets DIR, then toggles STEP with **`time.sleep_us(3)`** around each pulse inside `_step()` (see around lines 958–970 in `pico_control_script.py`). That is still software timing on the interpreter/CPU path, not PIO.

There are **no** `rp2.PIO`, `StateMachine`, or `@asm_pio`-style definitions in the firmware you have in-tree.

So compared to your target architecture (“PIO only generates STEP timing”), **execution is not hardware-timed PIO yet**; it’s the fixed-period main loop plus GPIO/sleep for pulses.

[REDACTED]

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
ENTRY 8  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
**No.** Nothing in your firmware uses the Pico’s PIO blocks.

Stepping is done in MicroPython on the CPU:

- **28BYJ** — Four GPIOs driven from a half-step table in `_step()`; cadence tied to the main `STEP_US` loop.
- **STEP/DIR** — DIR on GPIO, STEP toggled with `time.sleep_us(...)` in `_step()` — still software delays, not PIO.

So there is no `rp2.PIO`, no `StateMachine`, and no PIO assembler programs in the current design.

ENTRY 16  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s how **this project** treats PC → Pico streaming, and what that implies for **speed vs reliability**.

### What the Windows app actually caps

`MainWindow._hardware_angle_stream_min_interval_s()` ([`gui.py`](Robot-Arm-Sim/gui.py)) picks a **maximum line rate** for `J0:…,J1:…\n` frames from a **nominal bandwidth hint** (`iface._baud`), not from measuring the link.

- **USB (`PicoInterface`)** — `_baud` comes from [`BAUD_RATE` = 115200](Robot-Arm-Sim/hardware/protocol.py) unless you change it at connect time.  
  With the built‑in formula (`~10 bits/byte`, `est_bytes ≈ 2 + n×15`, joint count `n = 1 + num_planar_joints`), a **4‑joint** line is ~62 bytes → **on the order of ~100–130 Hz** sustained **before** the animation caps (animation clamps Wi‑Fi to **330 Hz max**, USB stays bounded by that serial budget).

- **Wi‑Fi TCP (`PicoWifiInterface`)** — `_baud` is a **fake “921600”** hint ([`pico_wifi_interface.py`](Robot-Arm-Sim/hardware/pico_wifi_interface.py)): LAN is not 921600 UART. That makes the formula allow **up to ~330 Hz** lines **while animating** (`min(330, …)`). Idle motion uses a **lower cap** (`min(200, …)` branch without the 330 ceiling).

So: **USB is realistically ~100+ Hz** for typical joint counts at 115200; **Wi‑Fi in-app targeting can be much higher (hundreds of Hz)** but that’s **not** a guarantee the Pico always keeps up—it's a **budget**.

### Reliability (what actually bites you)

| Factor | Effect |
|--------|--------|
| **TCP over Wi‑Fi** | Throughput is usually fine; **delay jitter** is the issue. Latency spikes mean the Pico sees bursts or gaps unless you buffer (your planned 20–50 ms buffer). |
| **TX queue** (`maxsize=32`) | If the PC outruns drain, **oldest frames are dropped** — motion can skip or jump unless the protocol tolerates it. |
| **MicroPython main loop** | The Pico services USB/Wi‑Fi and stepping in one loop (`STEP_US`). Heavy work or Wi‑Fi spikes can **stretch loop time**; docs already cap motion `dt` partly for that. |
| **USB serial** | More **predictable** throughput than Wi‑Fi if you stay under ~real UART bandwidth; still subject to OS scheduling and CDC buffering. |

### Practical takeaway

- **“How fast?”** — With **current code**: **~100–130 Hz** is a sensible mental model for **USB @ 115200** and multi‑joint lines; **Wi‑Fi** can be configured by the GUI logic to **approach hundreds of Hz**, but that’s **throughput-oriented**, not jitter-proof.  
- **“How reliable?”** — **USB** is usually **more deterministic** for steady streaming; **Wi‑Fi** is **usable** but **less stable in time** unless you add buffering and tolerate drops (or use a tighter rate + binary framing later).

For **50–200 Hz** trajectory streaming (your CNC-style goal), **USB at 115200 is already in range**; **Wi‑Fi can work in that band** if you avoid pegging 330 Hz blindly and add **lookahead + underrun handling** on the Pico.

Update rate is throttled by the caller.\n\nTrajectory mode (CNC-style buffering on firmware):\n    T:<stream_seconds>,J0:45.00,J1:32.50,...\\n\n    ``stream_seconds`` is monotonic time in seconds on the host stream timeline\n    (starts at 0 after connect / SYNC seed). burst; used right after connect so the real arm is not told to\n        “sprint” from a zeroed controller state to the simulator pose.\n\nMessage format (Pico → host, optional status):\n    OK\\n If no TCP session, return.\n2. `encode_trajectory_sample()` builds UTF-8 bytes: `T:` + six fractional digits + comma + `Ji:deg` tuples + `\\n`.\n3. Enqueue on `queue.Queue(maxsize=32)`. Stepper firmware converts targets to step counts and follows with accel-limited stepping toward those targets in the periodic motor update.",

ENTRY 56  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here is what was implemented (plan file untouched):

### Protocol ([`Robot-Arm-Sim/hardware/protocol.py`](Robot-Arm-Sim/hardware/protocol.py))
- **`encode_trajectory_sample(angles_rad, stream_time_s)`** → `T:<6dp>,J0:…\n`
- **`clamp_trajectory_stream_hz`**, **`TRAJECTORY_STREAM_HZ`** (default **100**, clamped **50–200**)
- **`split_trajectory_prefix`** for parsing (host/tests)

### Host ([`pico_interface.py`](Robot-Arm-Sim/hardware/pico_interface.py), [`pico_wifi_interface.py`](Robot-Arm-Sim/hardware/pico_wifi_interface.py), [`gui.py`](Robot-Arm-Sim/gui.py))
- **`send_joint_trajectory_sample(angles_rad, stream_time_s)`** on both transports (queue depth 32, drop oldest on overflow)
- GUI streams **`T:`** poses at **`1 / TRAJECTORY_STREAM_HZ`**, using **`time.monotonic() - _hw_stream_origin_mono`** after **`_seed_hardware_pose_from_sim`** resets the origin

### Firmware ([`Robot-Arm-Sim/firmware/pico_control_script.py`](Robot-Arm-Sim/firmware/pico_control_script.py))
- **`TrajectoryRing`** + **`TRAJ_LOOKAHEAD_S ≈ 35 ms`**, linear interpolation, hold past latest knot / before earliest (underrun/hold)
- **`STOP`** → **`_traj_reset()`**; **`SYNC:`** → reset ring, apply pose, **`_traj_capture_prev_from_axes`**
- **`T:`** lines feed the ring; plain **`J0:…`** still maps to immediate **`set_target_angle`**
- **`_playback_traj_to_axes`** runs each loop **before** stepping
- **Jerk**: optional **`jerk`** (steps/s³) limits **`|Δ(dv)|`** per tick on top of accel shaping in **`_host_follow_budget`**
- **PIO STEP** for **`stepdir`**: **`StepperAxisStepDirPIO`** + asm program (wait → pulse); DIR still GPIO; **`pio_stepdir`** default **`True`**, falls back to **`StepperAxisStepDir`** if PIO fails; **28BYJ unchanged**

### Docs
- [`docs/NETWORKING_AND_COMMUNICATIONS.md`](docs/NETWORKING_AND_COMMUNICATIONS.md) — `T:` payload, rates, ring/STOP/SYNC, PIO/jerk
- [`docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`](docs/ARCHITECTURE.md) — APIs, main-loop order, animation/hardware note

**Tuning:** set **`TRAJECTORY_STREAM_HZ`** in [`protocol.py`](Robot-Arm-Sim/hardware/protocol.py); add **`"jerk": <steps_per_sec_cubed>`** per joint in **`JOINTS`** for smoother accel changes; set **`"pio_stepdir": false`** on a **`stepdir`** joint to force software STEP pulses.

**Deploy:** rebuild/upload **`pico_control_script.py`** to the Pico so the device matches the new PC protocol.

{
  "new_string": "            pass\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# Unified velocity → step budget (host-follow, homing, PIO pulse spacing)\n# All step-rate motion uses these limits only: max_speed_sps, accel_sps2, jerk_sps3.\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n\nAXIS_PLAN_EPS_STEPS = 0.5\nHOMING_PLAN_ERR_MAG = 1e9\n\n\ndef axis_plan_velocity_steps(\n    dt,\n    signed_position_error_steps,\n    vel_signed,\n    frac_carry,\n    dv_prev,\n    max_speed_sps,\n    accel_sps2,\n    jerk_sps3,\n    max_burst,\n):\n    \"\"\"Plan integer full-steps this tick from accel/jerk-limited velocity toward ``signed_position_error_steps``.\n\n    Parameters mirror motor tab units (full steps/s, s/s², s/s³). Returns:\n    ``(n_steps, new_vel_signed, new_frac_carry, new_dv_prev)``.\n    \"\"\"\n    tdt = max(dt, 1e-9)\n    e = float(signed_position_error_steps)\n    a = abs(e)\n    if a < AXIS_PLAN_EPS_STEPS:\n        return 0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0\n\n    ae = float(accel_sps2)\n    if ae <= 0.0:\n        ae = max(float(max_speed_sps) * 10.0, 400.0)\n\n    vmax = max(float(max_speed_sps), 1e-9)\n    ds = max(0.0, a - 0.5)\n    v_cap_k = math.sqrt(max(0.0, 2.0 * ae * ds))\n    v_cap = min(vmax, v_cap_k)\n    v_cmd = math.copysign(v_cap, e)\n\n    dv_max = ae * tdt\n    diff = v_cmd - vel_signed\n    if diff > dv_max:\n        dv_raw = dv_max\n    elif diff < -dv_max:\n        dv_raw = -dv_max\n    else:\n        dv_raw = diff\n\n    jk = float(jerk_sps3)\n    if jk > 0.0:\n        lim = jk * tdt * tdt\n        if dv_raw > dv_prev + lim:\n            dv_raw = dv_prev + lim\n        elif dv_raw < dv_prev - lim:\n            dv_raw = dv_prev - lim\n        ndv_prev = dv_raw\n    else:\n        ndv_prev = dv_raw\n\n    vel_signed = vel_signed + dv_raw\n    spd = abs(vel_signed)\n    if spd < 1e-9:\n        return 0, vel_signed, frac_carry, ndv_prev\n\n    frac_carry += spd * tdt\n    n_rate = int(frac_carry)\n    frac_carry -= float(n_rate)\n    n_rate = min(n_rate, int(max_burst))\n    n_want = int(min(a + 0.5, 1e7))\n    return min(n_want, n_rate), vel_signed, frac_carry, ndv_prev\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# Stepper axis base class\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------",

  "old_string": "    def _host_follow_budget(self, dt) -> int:\n        \"\"\"Steps to emit this tick toward host angle.\n\n        Trapezoid-like envelope: ``accel`` (steps/s², from Motor config) limits how fast\n        |velocity| can change; cruise speed is capped by ``max_sps`` and by sqrt(2*a*distance)\n        so we can decelerate to rest without overshooting the remaining error.\n        \"\"\"\n        e = self.target_pos - self.current_pos\n        a = abs(e)\n        tdt = max(dt, 1e-9)\n        if a < 0.5:\n            self._host_sps_accum = 0.0\n            self._host_follow_vel = 0.0\n            self._host_follow_frac = 0.0\n            return 0\n\n        ae = float(self.accel)\n        # max speed that can be killed in distance ~a at deceleration ae (kinematic cap)\n        ds = max(0.0, a - 0.5)\n        v_cap = math.sqrt(max(0.0, 2.0 * ae * ds)) if ae > 0.0 else float(self.max_sps)\n        v_cap = min(float(self.max_sps), v_cap)\n        v_cmd = math.copysign(v_cap, e)\n\n        dv_max = ae * tdt\n        v = self._host_follow_vel\n        diff = v_cmd - v\n        if diff > dv_max:\n            dv_raw = dv_max\n        elif diff < -dv_max:\n            dv_raw = -dv_max\n        else:\n            dv_raw = diff\n\n        jk = float(self.jerk)\n        if jk > 0.0:\n            dv_prev = self._host_dv_prev\n            lim = jk * tdt * tdt\n            if dv_raw > dv_prev + lim:\n                dv_raw = dv_prev + lim\n            elif dv_raw < dv_prev - lim:\n                dv_raw = dv_prev - lim\n            self._host_dv_prev = dv_raw\n        else:\n            self._host_dv_prev = dv_raw\n\n        v = v + dv_raw\n        self._host_follow_vel = v\n\n        spd = abs(v)\n        if spd < 1e-9:\n            return 0\n\n        self._host_follow_frac += spd * tdt\n        n_rate = int(self._host_follow_frac)\n        self._host_follow_frac -= float(n_rate)\n        n_rate = min(n_rate, MAX_STEPS_PER_AXIS_TICK)\n        n_want = int(min(a + 0.5, 1e7))\n        return min(n_want, n_rate)",

ENTRY 96  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was consolidated and how it behaves now.

### Before (multiple independent speed paths on the device)

1. **`_host_follow_budget`** – full accel/jerk/√(2ad) logic toward **`target_pos`**
2. **`_homing_step_budget`** – separate fractional accumulator at fixed **`home_*_speed_sps`**, ignoring **`accel`/`jerk`**
3. **`StepperAxisStepDirPIO._step`** – PIO period from **`max_sps` only**, not the live commanded velocity

### After: one planner on the Pico

**[`axis_plan_velocity_steps`](Robot-Arm-Sim/firmware/pico_control_script.py)** (module-level) is the **only** place that turns **`max_speed_sps`, `accel_sps2`, `jerk_sps3`** into a per-tick step count and signed velocity state.

- **`_host_follow_budget`** – thin wrapper: **`signed_error = target_pos - current_pos`**, limits **`max_sps`, `accel`, `jerk`**
- **`_homing_step_budget(speed_sps, dt, step_sign)`** – same planner with a large synthetic **`signed_error`** and **`max_speed_sps = speed_sps`** (search/fine), still using the joint’s **`accel`/`jerk`**
- **`StepperAxisStepDirPIO._step`** – spacing uses **`abs(_host_follow_vel)`** from that planner (fallback **`≥ 1` step/s**)

Shared integrators (**`_host_follow_vel`**, **`_host_follow_frac`**, **`_host_dv_prev`**) are cleared via **`clear_host_follow_rates()`** on STOP, SYNC, homing stage changes, and when starting HOME/HOMEJ.

**Removed:** **`_homing_step_accum`** and the old homing rate accumulator.

### PC side: one place for limit scaling → animation

New **[`hardware/motion_limits.py`](Robot-Arm-Sim/hardware/motion_limits.py)**:

- **`StepMotionLimits`**, **`host_follow_scale_factor`**, **`step_motion_limits_per_joint`**, **`rad_velocity_accel_from_step_limits`**

**[`animation.motor_rad_limits_from_joint_cfgs`](Robot-Arm-Sim/animation.py)** now delegates to that module so **`HOST_FOLLOW_MAX_SPS`** scaling matches the firmware cap logic without duplicating the math.

### Still separate (by design)

- **`_apply_host_follow_sps_cap`** at boot – scales **`JOINTS`** **`max_sps`/`accel`** when **`HOST_FOLLOW_MAX_SPS`** is set; the planner **only** reads the resulting **`self.max_sps`/`self.accel`/`self.jerk`**
- **`animation.min_move_duration_1d`** – wall-clock duration bound for **smoothstep** paths (ω/α), not step emission

So: **one motion planner function on the MCU** for stepped motion speed, and **one module on the PC** for the same numeric limits when converting to rad/s for the simulator.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 125  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was added and how it behaves.

### Hardware panel
- New checkbox: **“Wi‑Fi + web server in firmware”** (default **on**).
  - **On:** Same as before — if SSID is non-empty, deploy injects `ENABLE_WIFI = True` (Wi‑Fi, TCP, HTTP).
  - **Off:** Deploy always injects `ENABLE_WIFI = False` (USB-only build), even if SSID/password are filled in.
- Tooltip explains the tradeoff and that **over-the-air Deploy needs this on**.
- If you’re in **WiFi** connection mode and click Deploy with this **off**, a **warning** tells you to enable it or use USB deploy.

### Deployer (`micropython_deployer.py`)
- `_inject_wifi_config(..., embed_wifi=True)` — when `embed_wifi` is false, `ENABLE_WIFI` is forced off.
- `deploy(..., embed_wifi=True)` and `deploy_wifi(..., arm_config=None, embed_wifi=True)` take that flag from the GUI.

Also fixed **`deploy_wifi()`**: it used `arm_config` but it wasn’t in the signature (would have raised `NameError`). It now declares **`arm_config`** like `deploy()`.

### Saved layout
- Checkbox state is stored under **`firmware_include_wifi`** in the hardware panel save data (defaults to on when the key is missing).

**Using wireless / web:** leave the checkbox **on**, set SSID/password, deploy over USB first (or use WiFi deploy once the board already has Wi‑Fi firmware). For maximum simplicity on USB only, turn it **off** before Deploy.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 138  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s how this works and what we changed.

### Already true on the Pico
Wireless firmware runs the **same command handler** over TCP as over USB. The main loop drains `_wifi_rx` every tick, so once Wi‑Fi is up you **do not** need USB for control — only **power**.

### Hardware constraint (important)
Unplugging the **USB cable from the Pico** usually **removes power** unless something else feeds the board (another USB cable to power-only, bench supply on **VSYS**, battery pack, etc.). The laptop can be unplugged from USB **only if** the Pico still has 5 V.

### GUI / workflow changes
1. **USB deploy + wireless firmware + saved IP**  
   If **“Wi‑Fi + web server in firmware”** is on, SSID is set, and the **IP field** already has the Pico’s address, the app **no longer forces a USB reconnect** after deploy. It waits ~12 s and runs **Connect over Wi‑Fi** to that IP — same idea as after an over‑the‑air deploy. Terminal text explains that USB is optional **after** Wi‑Fi works and reminds you about **keeping the Pico powered**.

2. **First time (no IP yet)**  
   Behavior is unchanged: **USB reconnect after 4 s** so you can see `WiFi connected: x.x.x.x`, paste it into the IP field, then use **Wi‑Fi mode → Connect** (or the next USB deploy with IP filled will use the Wi‑Fi auto-connect path).

3. **Firmware**  
   - Docstring note: unplugging USB is OK for **communication** if power remains.  
   - Wi‑Fi command processing is gated with **`ENABLE_WIFI and WIFI_SSID`** so it matches when the Wi‑Fi thread actually runs.

4. **Checkbox tooltip**  
   Adds the **power / VSYS** note.

**Practical sequence:** Deploy wireless firmware over USB once, note IP → put IP in the panel → next deploy auto-connects via Wi‑Fi after boot → select **Wi‑Fi** in Hardware so the UI matches → you can unplug the **laptop–Pico** USB data/power cable only if the Pico is **still powered** another way.

ENTRY 152  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was going wrong.

### 1. **`main.py` never ran — syntax error**
In `StepperAxisStepDirPIO._step`, the `period = max(64, min(...))` expression had **bad parentheses** (`)))),` / extra `)`). That makes the whole file **invalid Python**, so MicroPython never finishes loading `main.py` and you only see the normal REPL banner.

That’s now corrected and the file passes `py_compile`.

### 2. **`dict | None` return annotation**
`parse_command` used `-> dict | None`, which some MicroPython builds choke on. The return annotation is removed so parsing stays safe.

### 3. **PIO init**
The PIO block now uses **`except Exception`** instead of only `ImportError`, so odd failures during `@asm_pio` / setup still fall back to ordinary GPIO stepping instead of killing import.

---

**What you should do:** Deploy again from the GUI (or your usual flow). You should see **`Robot Arm Pico Controller ready`** / **`firmware ready`** after reset. If anything still fails, open the serial REPL and run `import main` — MicroPython will print the traceback for any remaining error.

[REDACTED]

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
ENTRY 165  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here's what was going on:

### Why you saw `OK` all the time
1. **Protocol** — For each accepted `T:…` / `J0:…` line, the Pico answers with `OK\n`. That’s normal.
2. **Host behavior** — With **hardware synchronization** on, the GUI was sending trajectory samples at a **fixed rate** (about 50–200 Hz) even when the commanded pose **didn’t change**. So you got a steady stream of lines and matching `OK`s even though nothing was moving.
3. **Hardware panel** — The green **“Pico RX: OK …”** line was updated on **every** `OK`, while the terminal only logged `OK` at 1 Hz. So the panel looked “flooded” even when the terminal didn’t.

### What we changed
1. **Skip redundant sends** — After applying backlash, we compare to the last sent pose with `angles_changed()` from `protocol.py` (same threshold as elsewhere). If nothing changed, we **don’t** send another line, so the Pico doesn’t keep replying `OK` while idle.
2. **Rate-limit the Hardware panel** — That status label only refreshes at **1 Hz** for `OK`, like the terminal.

After a **SYNC / seed** (`_seed_hardware_pose_from_sim`), `_last_hw_streamed_adj` is cleared so the **first** sample always goes out; then idle duplicates are skipped until the pose moves again.

During **motion** you’ll still see `OK` often — one per command — because commands are actually changing.

[REDACTED]

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
ENTRY 166  ROLE: user
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
<user_query>
how to stop the OK stream while nothing is moving
</user_query>

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
ENTRY 167  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s how that behaves and how you can stop it.

### What stops idle `OK`s (in current code)
With **hardware synchronization** on, the PC **does not re-send** the same pose: it uses `angles_changed()` so while the commanded joints are unchanged, **no new lines go to the Pico**, so **no extra `OK`s** from those idle repeats.

So if you still see `OK` when “nothing is moving,” usually either:
- the sim is still **changing numbers slightly** (animation, drift, gravity offsets, backlash shaping), or  
- something else is talking to the Pico (terminal commands, another app).

### Ways to stop / reduce `OK` traffic yourself
1. **Turn off “Enable hardware synchronization”** in the Hardware panel — no steady `J0:…` / `T:…` stream, so no steady `OK`s from the sim (homing / LED / manual commands can still produce occasional replies).
2. **Disconnect** from the Pico — no stream at all.
3. **Leave sync on but pause motion** — once pose is stable, the duplicate-send skip should keep idle traffic off; if `OK` continues, something is still changing the commanded angles each tick.

### If you want zero streaming except when you jog
Use sync **off** for bench testing, then turn it **on** only when you want the arm to follow the simulation.

No extra setting is required beyond **sync off** or **disconnect** to stop the idle stream; the duplicate-pose skip handles the “sync on but idle” case in software.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 195  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s **why Wi‑Fi often “never connects”** on Pico W / Pico 2 W, and **what we changed** in your project.

### Common real-world causes
1. **`wlan.connect()` only in a background thread** — On CYW43 this often never finishes. The firmware now runs **association on the main thread**, then starts the TCP accept loop in a thread.
2. **Missing `rp2.country()`** — Many builds need a **2-letter ISO country code** before joining an AP. Deploy now injects **`WIFI_COUNTRY`** (default **`US`** via the new **Wi‑Fi country** field in the Hardware panel). Set it to your region (**GB**, **DE**, **JP**, etc.).
3. **2.4 GHz only** — The Pico 2 W radio does **not** use 5 GHz–only networks. Use a **2.4 GHz** SSID (or a router that exposes 2.4 GHz on that SSID).
4. **Wrong password / WPA3-only quirks** — Prefer **WPA2-Personal**. Some WPA3-only setups still fail on embedded stacks.
5. **Boot order** — Blocking Wi‑Fi for up to **~30 s** used to happen **before** `firmware ready`, which broke USB deploy/connect probes. Wi‑Fi join now runs **after** the usual boot banners so serial still comes up immediately.

### What you should do
1. **Deploy again** with **Wi‑Fi + web server** checked, correct **SSID/password**, and **Wi‑Fi country** = your country (not necessarily “US”).
2. Watch USB serial: you should see  
   `WiFi: joining YourSSID ...`  
   then either **`WiFi connected: x.x.x.x`** or **`WiFi connect failed status=…`** (that status code helps narrow router vs password vs AP-not-found).
3. Put that IP in the Hardware panel for Wi‑Fi mode.

If it still fails after deploy, paste the **exact** `WiFi connect failed status=…` line (or a screenshot of the serial log from `WiFi: joining` onward).

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 207  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s **why you might never see an IP**, and what we changed.

### Why `WiFi connected: …` might never appear
1. **Association never completes** — wrong password, WPA3-only AP, 5 GHz-only SSID, bad country code, etc.
2. **`isconnected()` was true but DHCP hadn’t run** — `ifconfig()` stayed `0.0.0.0`, so you effectively had no usable IP and behavior looked broken.
3. **Boot logs missed** — Wi‑Fi runs **after** `firmware ready`, can take **up to ~60 s**, and progress wasn’t obvious on serial.

### Firmware updates (re‑deploy to get them)
1. **Wait for a real address** — Loops until `ifconfig()[0]` is set and **not** `0.0.0.0` (not only `isconnected()`).
2. **Longer wait** — Up to **~60 s** with a **status line every 5 s**:  
   `WiFi: waiting status=… (5s) …`
3. **Clearer boot lines** — `WiFi: rp2.country('XX') OK`, `WiFi: ssid='…'`, `WiFi: joining …`, then either **`WiFi connected: a.b.c.d`** or **`WiFi connect failed status=…`**.
4. **`wlan.disconnect()`** before `connect()` so stale sessions don’t block.
5. **`sys.stdout.flush()`** after important prints so USB serial sees them quickly.
6. **Serial command `WIFI`** — If you’re connected over USB (terminal), type **`WIFI`** (the GUI already forwards this when hardware is connected). The Pico answers with **`WiFi connected: …`** or **`WiFi: not connected status=…`**.

### Serial parser fix (host)
Lines starting with **`WiFi:`** are now treated as firmware output (not REPL), same as `WiFi `.

---

**What you should do:** Deploy this firmware, watch USB serial from **`WiFi: joining`** onward for **60 s**, note any **`WiFi: waiting status=N`** / **`WiFi connect failed status=N`**.  
If you’re already connected on USB, send **`WIFI`** once to force the IP line again.

Typical **`status`** hints on RP2/CYW43: **`-1`** wrong password, **`-2`** AP not found (often 5 GHz-only or SSID typo), **`-3`** generic failure. Match **Wi‑Fi country** to your region and use a **2.4 GHz WPA2** network when possible.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 222  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was going wrong:

### Cause
Boot order was:

1. Print **`firmware ready`**
2. Enter **`_wifi_sta_connect_blocking()`** for up to **~60 s**
3. Only **after that** start the main **`while True:`** loop that reads USB stdin

The PC connect probe sends **`J0:…`** right after it sees the banner. During the Wi‑Fi wait **nothing was reading stdin**, so the Pico never answered **`OK`** and the host assumed you were on the REPL.

### Fix (in `pico_control_script.py`)
1. **`_handle_line` is defined before the banners** so command handling exists as soon as USB is up.
2. **`_poll_stdio_commands_nonblocking()`** drives the same line protocol as the main loop (poll + assemble lines + `_handle_line`).
3. **`_wifi_sta_connect_blocking(_poller, _handle_line, usb_buf_cell)`** runs that poll **right after `wlan.connect()`** and **on every 100 ms wait iteration**, and keeps the USB line buffer in sync with **`buf`** for when the main loop starts.

Redeploy firmware and connect again; the **`J0:`** probe should get **`OK`** even while Wi‑Fi is still joining.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 235  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was wrong:

### Cause
`PicoWifiInterface` waits on the **TCP socket** for the strings `firmware ready` / `Robot Arm Pico Controller ready`. Those lines were only printed to **USB stdout**, not written to the socket. After a TCP connect the buffer stayed empty until you sent a command, so the client hit the timeout every time.

### Changes
1. **Firmware (`pico_control_script.py`)** — On each new TCP client, the server now sends:
   - `Robot Arm Pico Controller ready\n`
   - `firmware ready\n`  
   so Wi‑Fi connect matches what USB sees.

2. **Wi‑Fi deploy (`micropython_deployer.py`)** — The upload client used to assume the first line after `UPLOAD_BEGIN` was `UPLOAD_READY`. With the banner first, that broke. It now **reads lines until `UPLOAD_READY`**, skipping those two banner lines (and empty reads), with bounded read timeouts.

Redeploy firmware (USB is fine), reboot the Pico, then **Connect** over Wi‑Fi again to `192.168.86.133:8888`.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 247  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was tightened so Wi‑Fi connect stays reliable end‑to‑end.

### Changes in `pico_wifi_interface.py`

1. **Banner detection**  
   Success now requires the substring **`firmware ready`** (not only `Robot Arm Pico Controller ready`). That avoids treating a half‑banner as “ready” when the second line hasn’t arrived yet.

2. **No dropped TCP bytes**  
   Anything read **after** `firmware ready` is stored in `_rx_carryover`. The **J0 probe** merges that with a short kernel drain and the **OK** response into one buffer, then hands it to **`_rx_loop`**. Previously, bytes after the banner could be read once and thrown away, which could break the probe or lose early `WiFi:` lines.

3. **PING fallback**  
   If there’s still no banner after **3 s**, the client sends **`PING\n`** once. Firmware answers with the same two banner lines over TCP, which helps if the initial `sendall` on accept was delayed or lost.

4. **Timeout**  
   **`CONNECT_TIMEOUT`** is **45 s** so slow STA join still has time after TCP connects.

5. **Cleanup**  
   **`_rx_carryover`** is cleared on failed banner/probe, **`disconnect`**, and at the start of **`connect`**.

The firmware-not-responding error text now matches the stricter rule (must see **`firmware ready`**).

**What you should do on hardware:** redeploy `pico_control_script.py`, then try **USB Connect** and **Wi‑Fi Connect** (banner → **J0** → **OK**). With these host fixes plus your firmware TCP banner and stdin polling during Wi‑Fi join, that path should be consistent.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 270  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what changed—**28BYJ is unchanged** (same class, `make_axis` branch, defaults, STEPT paths).

### NEMA (`stepdir`) and PIO

- **`pio_stepdir` default stays `True`.** With that, the firmware **always** builds **`StepperAxisStepDirPIO`** — there is **no** silent fallback to **`StepperAxisStepDir`** if PIO setup throws.
- **If PIO isn’t available** (`_PIO_HAVE_STEP` false), **`make_axis` raises** with a message to use **`pio_stepdir: false`** only for GPIO STEP debugging (so NEMA still works on broken/unusual builds if you opt in).
- **Explicit opt-out:** `"pio_stepdir": false` in that joint’s JOINTS dict still selects **`StepperAxisStepDir`** (GPIO toggling). Nothing in your deploy path sets that today.

Docs/comments at the top of the file, the PIO comment block, **MOTORINFO**, and the main-loop comment were adjusted so they match this behavior. **`StepperAxisStepDirPIO._step`** was already the only place that emits STEP pulses for the PIO path (`sm.put`); that’s unchanged.

Redeploy firmware after pulling these edits so the Pico picks up the new `make_axis` logic.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 284  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was going wrong and what we changed.

### Why TCP looked “random” (banner timeout after a good connect)

The Pico only accepts **one** Wi‑Fi control client at a time: it keeps a single `conn` and **does not call `accept()` again** until that slot is empty.

On `recv()`, **non‑timeout errors** (common after a **RST**, flaky Wi‑Fi, or the PC closing the socket oddly) were caught with `except OSError: pass`, so the socket was **never cleared**. The loop kept thinking a client was still connected, so:

- New TCP connections could complete on your PC, but the Pico **never moved on to accept/serve them**, or  
- You’d get a stuck half‑open state with **no `firmware ready` lines**.

So the first connect could work; a later one could fail even though the IP was right.

### Fixes in `pico_control_script.py`

1. **`recv` errors** — Treat **EAGAIN / EWOULDBLOCK / ETIMEDOUT** (and **11 / 116** on some lwIP builds) as “no data this loop”. For **anything else**, **close and clear** `conn` so the next client can connect and get the banner again.

2. **TCP `listen(1)` → `listen(4)`** — Slightly larger backlog so brief reconnects are less likely to be dropped.

3. **HTTP `listen(2)` → `listen(4)`** — Same idea for the dashboard.

4. **Boot logs** — After bind you’ll see  
   `TCP control listening on port 8888`  
   and  
   `HTTP dashboard listening on port 8080`  
   so on serial you can confirm both threads actually started.

**Redeploy** this firmware to the Pico.

### Web UI

Use **`http://192.168.86.133:8080`** (your Pico IP + **`WEB_HTTP_PORT`**, default **8080**).  
If it still doesn’t load: check the serial log for **`HTTP dashboard listening`** and **`WARN: web dashboard:`** (that prints if the HTTP thread dies, e.g. bind failure).

### Still flaky?

Then it’s likely **Wi‑Fi/router** (Weak signal, AP isolation, printer‑guest VLAN, etc.). The TCP slot bug above was a real firmware foot‑gun that matched “connected once, then weird/no banner”; fixing that should make reconnects much more reliable after deploy.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 286  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Those replies mean **your network is telling you it cannot deliver packets to `192.168.86.133`**. The box answering (`192.168.86.56`) is almost certainly **your PC or your router** on that LAN saying “I don’t have a route / ARP entry for that host” — not a problem with the Robot Arm app itself.

### Most likely: **stale IP**
The Pico’s address from DHCP **changes**. The `.133` you saved earlier may no longer be assigned.

**Do this:**

1. **USB serial to the Pico** (Thonny or any terminal) and reboot it — watch for  
   `WiFi connected: x.x.x.x`  
   or send `WIFI` and note the IP.

2. **Router admin → DHCP / connected devices** — find “Raspberry Pi” / “CYW43” / hostname you recognize and use **that** IP.

3. Ping **that** current IP again.

### If ping still fails after you have the fresh IP

| Check | Why |
|--------|-----|
| **Same Wi‑Fi SSID** as the Pico | Different SSID or guest network → isolation or different subnet |
| **AP / client isolation** off | Some routers block Wi‑Fi ↔ Wi‑Fi; ping never works between laptop and Pico |
| **VPN off** on the PC | Can steal routes |
| **Firewall** | Rarely blocks ping *to* another LAN host; isolation is more common |

### Quick sanity check on the PC

```text
ipconfig
```

Confirm your Wi‑Fi adapter is **`192.168.86.x`** (same `/24` as the Pico). If your PC is on another subnet, you’ll never reach `.133` until they share the same LAN.

---

**Bottom line:** Treat **`192.168.86.133` as unverified until you read the live IP from USB or the router.** Once ping works to the **current** IP, TCP `8888` and HTTP `8088`/`8080` can work too.

ENTRY 307  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was going wrong and what we changed.

### 1. **USB after deploy (GUI)**

Previously, if **Wi‑Fi credentials + saved IP** were set, the app **only** scheduled a Wi‑Fi TCP reconnect after ~12 s and **skipped** USB serial reconnect — so it felt like you were “kicked off” USB after a flash.

**Now:**

- **USB radio selected:** After a successful USB deploy, the GUI **always** schedules USB reconnect in ~4 s again (same COM port). The Pico can still join Wi‑Fi and serve HTTP/TCP in parallel; you **don’t** need to unplug USB for that.
- **Wi‑Fi radio selected** with an IP: Still auto‑connects over TCP after ~12 s.
- **Wi‑Fi radio selected** with **no** IP: After ~4 s it reconnects USB as a fallback and tells you to set the IP (or switch back to USB).

The serial port **must** be closed **only while** the deploy tools (`mpremote` / raw REPL) have exclusive access — that’s unavoidable — but after flashing it comes back automatically when **USB** mode is selected.

---

### 2. **Browser “connection refused” on HTTP (firmware)**

Likely causes we addressed:

- **Thread stack:** Raised `_thread.stack_size(12 * 1024)` before starting the TCP and HTTP threads so the HTTP handler is less likely to crash silently on MicroPython.
- **Bind address:** Some **CYW43 / lwIP** setups don’t accept LAN traffic on `0.0.0.0` reliably. The HTTP server now tries **`(STA_IP, 8080)` first**, then `0.0.0.0`, then `""`.
- **TCP control:** Same idea for port **8888** — try **`(STA_IP, 8888)`** first, then `""`.
- **Diagnostics:** If the HTTP thread fails to start, it prints **`WARN: web dashboard thread:`** plus **`sys.print_exception`**. On success you’ll see **`HTTP dashboard listening on …`** with the actual bind address.

**Redeploy** this firmware, then on serial confirm:

- `WiFi connected: …`
- `TCP control listening on …`
- `HTTP dashboard listening on …`
- `HTTP dashboard (same LAN): http://<ip>:8080`

Use **`http://<that-ip>:8080`** (not HTTPS).

---

### 3. **If the site still refuses**

Then it’s usually **layer‑3**, not the app: wrong/stale IP (`ping` must work), Pico not on the same LAN/guest isolation, or Wi‑Fi not embedded (`ENABLE_WIFI` + SSID in deploy). Serial lines above tell you which case it is.

---

**Files touched:** `Robot-Arm-Sim/gui.py` (`_on_deploy_finished`), `Robot-Arm-Sim/firmware/pico_control_script.py` (HTTP/TCP bind + thread stack).

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 319  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s how this matches what you described and what we changed in the GUI.

### What actually happens (Pico vs GUI)

- **TCP `:8888` and HTTP `:8080`** are started by **MicroPython on the Pico** after Wi‑Fi joins (`ENABLE_WIFI` + SSID in the deployed firmware). The desktop app does **not** open those sockets; it only flashes `main.py` and optionally connects **its own** TCP client for the simulator.
- While USB is plugged in, the Pico can run **USB CDC serial and Wi‑Fi at the same time** — that’s normal for Pico 2 W.
- If you **remove USB data** but **keep power** (charger, VSYS, powered hub, etc.), STA mode stays up and **`http://<pico-ip>:8080`** keeps working from the browser as long as the radio still has an IP on your LAN.

### GUI behavior updates (`gui.py`)

1. **After a USB (REPL) deploy**, the GUI **always** schedules **USB serial reconnect in ~4 s**, whether you picked **USB** or **Wi‑Fi** in the Hardware panel. So you stay on the COM terminal while plugged in; Wi‑Fi/HTTP on the board are independent.
2. **Wi‑Fi radio with no IP** no longer errors — it **falls through to USB flashing**, with a short log line explaining that OTA needs an IP later.
3. **Wi‑Fi OTA** (radio Wi‑Fi **with** IP) is unchanged: brief TCP disconnect for upload, then auto TCP reconnect at 12 s; logs now say the **browser dashboard** is **`http://<saved-ip>:8080`** and still works when USB is unplugged if the Pico stays powered.
4. **Docs in code**: `_on_hardware_deploy` docstring updated so it matches this USB vs OTA split.

### Simulator vs browser

The simulator still uses **one** hardware link at a time (`_hardware`). After a **USB** deploy we reconnect **USB** so the terminal works. For **Wi‑Fi-only** simulator control after you move the arm off USB, use **Hardware → Connect** (TCP). The **browser** never needed that — it talks straight to the Pico.

Deploy the updated GUI behavior with your next build; no firmware change required for this part beyond what you already have for Wi‑Fi + HTTP on the Pico.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 321  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
It’s all **inside the Pico firmware file**, not a separate HTML file on disk in the repo.

### Where it lives

```441:496:c:\Users\maxwe\OneDrive\Documents\GitHub\Robot Arm Sim\Robot-Arm-Sim\firmware\pico_control_script.py
def _web_http_server_thread(sta_ip):
    """HTTP dashboard on WEB_HTTP_PORT. sta_ip = WLAN IPv4 (helps bind on CYW43)."""
    global _http_axes_ref
    try:
        import json
        import socket

        port = int(WEB_HTTP_PORT)
        ...
        html = """<!DOCTYPE html><html><head><meta charset="utf-8"/>...
```

- **Server:** `_web_http_server_thread()` in  
  `Robot-Arm-Sim/firmware/pico_control_script.py`  
  It binds to **`WEB_HTTP_PORT`** (default **8080**, set near the `WEB_HTTP_PORT = 8080` block around lines 227–237).

- **HTML + UI:** The **`html = """..."""`** triple-quoted string in that same function — single embedded document with inline CSS and JavaScript.

### Behavior (real-time jog + pose)

- **`/` / `/index.html`** → serves that HTML page.
- **`/api/state`** → JSON with joint angles, FK polyline, EE position, switches — polled every **250 ms** from the browser (`setInterval(poll, 250)` in the embedded `<script>`).
- **`/api/jog_joint?j=<idx>&delta=<deg>`** → queues a jog on the Pico (`_web_cmd_queue`).
- **`/api/jog_ee?dx=...`** → end-effector jog via onboard IK.

Commands are applied in the main loop via `_web_cmd_queue` and `_apply_web_command()` elsewhere in the same file.

So: **deploy that firmware** → the Pico runs this MicroPython HTTP server and serves **that inline HTML**; there is no separate `index.html` file in the project unless you extract it yourself.

ENTRY 374  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was finished to match **USB-only desktop ↔ Pico** and **wireless control only via the HTTP dashboard**.

### `gui.py`
- **Removed Wi‑Fi OTA deploy**: no `deploy_wifi` path, no `_poll_wifi`, no `_on_deploy_finished_wifi` (so nothing calls the removed `_on_hardware_wifi_connect`).
- **`_on_hardware_deploy`**: docstring is USB-only; deploy error text no longer mentions connecting over Wi‑Fi to deploy.
- **`_on_deploy_finished`**: dropped the broken `is_wifi_mode()` branch (that method no longer exists on the panel). One **embed + SSID** message now explains **browser at `http://<pico-ip>:8080`**, optional firmware TCP, and **this app stays on USB**.
- **SSID / checkbox tooltips**: removed “Wi‑Fi re-deploy”; checkbox text no longer claims GUI OTA over TCP.
- **`SETPORT` / `BROADCAST`**: copy clarifies firmware TCP / discovery only; **`BROADCAST` uses `get_wifi_port()`** instead of hard-coded `8888`.

### `sim_terminal/help_lines.py`
- **CONNECT**, **WIFI**, **SETPORT**, **BROADCAST**, **DEPLOY** lines updated for USB + browser workflow; added a short note that wireless control is **`http://<pico-ip>:8080`** in a browser.

`python -m py_compile` on `gui.py` and `help_lines.py` succeeds.

`MicroPythonDeployer.deploy_wifi` and `PicoWifiInterface` remain for scripts or other tools; the **GUI no longer opens a wireless TCP session** to the Pico for deploy or control.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 378  ROLE: assistant

The **desktop simulator does not connect to the Pico’s web server.** Wireless control is **you + a normal browser** talking HTTP to the Pico on your LAN.

### What actually connects

1. **Pico firmware** starts an HTTP listener after Wi‑Fi is up. It binds ```441:486:c:\Users\maxwe\OneDrive\Documents\GitHub\Robot Arm Sim\Robot-Arm-Sim\firmware\pico_control_script.py
def _web_http_server_thread(sta_ip):
    """HTTP dashboard on WEB_HTTP_PORT (STA mode only).
    ...
        srv.listen(4)
        ...
        print("HTTP dashboard listening on {}:{}".format(_show_ip, port))
```

   Default port is **`WEB_HTTP_PORT = 8080`** (injected with arm config).

2. **You open** `http://<pico-ip>:8080` in Chrome/Firefox/Edge on any device on the same network (often the same PC that runs the sim).

3. The **page’s JavaScript** uses **`fetch()`** against the Pico: it polls **`/api/state`** and sends jog actions to endpoints like **`/api/jog_joint`** and **`/api/jog_ee`** (see the embedded HTML/JS in the same function).

So the “connection” is plain **TCP port 8080 + HTTP** from the browser to the Pico — **not** a special link inside `gui.py`.

### How that relates to the simulator

- **Simulator ↔ Pico**: **USB serial** (Connect in Hardware / `CONNECT`), same as before.
- **Wireless jogging / live-ish UI**: **only via that browser session**, not via the PyQt app opening HTTP.

If something doesn’t load, check firewall, that the Pico printed **`HTTP dashboard listening on …:8080`**, and that you’re using the **STA IP** (often shown as `WiFi connected: …` on USB serial).

ENTRY 381  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s the distinction:

### Mechanically (what “TCP” means)

Any program on the PC that wants to talk to the Pico’s **command TCP port** (default **8888**, configurable at deploy) opens a normal **TCP client** socket to **`Pico_IP:port`**. In this repo that’s done with Python’s **`socket.create_connection((host, port))`** inside `PicoWifiInterface.connect()`:

```112:119:c:\Users\maxwe\OneDrive\Documents\GitHub\Robot Arm Sim\Robot-Arm-Sim\hardware\pico_wifi_interface.py
        # Try to connect, retrying until timeout (Pico WiFi join can take ~5 s)
        sock = None
        deadline = time.monotonic() + timeout
        last_exc = None
        while time.monotonic() < deadline:
            try:
                sock = socket.create_connection((host, port), timeout=3.0)
                break
```

After connect, it uses the same **TX/RX threads + queues** pattern as serial: lines go over the socket instead of USB CDC.

### In practice with **this desktop app**

The **GUI no longer uses that TCP path** for hardware control. The simulator talks to the Pico over **USB serial** (`PicoInterface`), not `PicoWifiInterface`.

TCP from the PC still shows up for:

- **`PicoWifiInterface`** — if *you* call it from your own script/tool (not wired through Connect anymore).
- **`MicroPythonDeployer.deploy_wifi`** — optional **OTA deploy** over TCP (GUI path was removed; deploy is USB from the app).
- **Terminal `BROADCAST`** — scans the LAN with **`socket.create_connection`** to see **who answers on that TCP port** (discovery only; it doesn’t replace Connect).

### Summary

| Path | How the computer connects |
|------|---------------------------|
| **Simulator ↔ Pico (current GUI)** | **USB serial** (COM port), not LAN TCP. |
| **LAN TCP :8888 (etc.)** | **`socket.create_connection`** (or any TCP client) to Pico IP while firmware has Wi‑Fi + TCP server enabled. |
| **Browser dashboard** | **HTTP on :8080**, not the command TCP port. |

So: **the computer “connects to the TCP port” only when some client code explicitly opens TCP to that IP and port**—the desktop app’s normal hardware link is **not** that anymore.

ENTRY 386  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
The **HTTP “web server” does not open the command TCP port.** They are **two separate TCP listeners**, started as **two different threads** after Wi‑Fi connects.

### 1. HTTP dashboard (`WEB_HTTP_PORT`, usually **8080**)

`_web_http_server_thread` creates its **own** socket: `socket` → `bind` → `listen` → loop with `accept()`. That’s the “web server” — plain TCP carrying HTTP request/response on port **8080**.

```441:466:c:\Users\maxwe\OneDrive\Documents\GitHub\Robot Arm Sim\Robot-Arm-Sim\firmware\pico_control_script.py
def _web_http_server_thread(sta_ip):
    """HTTP dashboard on WEB_HTTP_PORT (STA mode only).
    ...
        for addr in candidates:
            sock = None
            try:
                sock = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM)
                sock.setsockopt(socket.SOL_SOCKET, socket.SO_REUSEADDR, 1)
                sock.bind(addr)
```

### 2. Command / LAN TCP (`TCP_PORT`, usually **8888**)

`_wifi_tcp_loop` builds **another** socket the same way, but binds **`TCP_PORT`** — text commands, upload protocol, etc. Also `listen` + `accept`, unrelated socket.

```1970:2008:c:\Users\maxwe\OneDrive\Documents\GitHub\Robot Arm Sim\Robot-Arm-Sim\firmware\pico_control_script.py
            def _wifi_tcp_loop():
                port = TCP_PORT
                ...
                        tsock = _socket.socket()
                        tsock.setsockopt(_socket.SOL_SOCKET, _socket.SO_REUSEADDR, 1)
                        tsock.bind(taddr)
                        ...
                srv.listen(4)
```

### 3. How both get started

After STA gets an IP, firmware starts **both** threads: TCP control loop first, then HTTP thread.

```2150:2156:c:\Users\maxwe\OneDrive\Documents\GitHub\Robot Arm Sim\Robot-Arm-Sim\firmware\pico_control_script.py
            _thread_mod.start_new_thread(_wifi_tcp_loop, ())
            ...
                _thread_mod.start_new_thread(_web_http_server_thread, (_sta_ip,))
```

So: **opening the web server only opens port 8080.** Port **8888** is opened by **`_wifi_tcp_loop`**, not by the HTTP code. Same underlying stack (MicroPython `socket` / lwIP), **different ports and threads.**

ENTRY 389  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
From the firmware HTTP handler, the browser only talks over **HTTP GET** (the page uses `fetch(...)` with default GET). These are the paths that do anything useful:

### Pages / read-only API

| Request | What it does |
|--------|----------------|
| **`GET /`** or **`GET /index.html`** | Returns the embedded dashboard HTML (UI + JS). |
| **`GET /api/state`** | Returns **JSON**: joint angles, limit-switch bits, FK end-effector `ee` `{x,y,z}`, `polyline_xy` for the SVG arm sketch, `fk_ok`, plus `tcp_control_port` / `http_port`. Read-only snapshot. |

### “Commands” (motion requests)

| Request | Query params | Effect |
|---------|----------------|--------|
| **`GET /api/jog_joint`** | **`j`** = joint index (integer), **`delta`** = degrees (float) | Queues `("JJ", jn, delta)` → main loop adjusts that joint’s target by `delta` °. |
| **`GET /api/jog_ee`** | **`dx`** = mm along **world X** (float) | Queues `("JE", dx)` → onboard IK moves the EE by `dx` mm (same idea as the EE −X / +X buttons). |

Responses for those APIs are JSON `{"ok":true}` or `{"ok":false}` on parse errors.

### Anything else

Any other path gets **`Not found`** (plain text), still HTTP 200 in the current code.

So in practice the browser **does not** send arbitrary serial-style text commands over HTTP—only **`/api/jog_joint`** and **`/api/jog_ee`** as GETs with query strings, plus **`/api/state`** for telemetry. Full command sets (HOME, STEPT, trajectory lines, etc.) stay on **USB** or the **TCP command port**, not this tiny HTTP API.

ENTRY 392  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
From the embedded page in `pico_control_script.py`, here’s what you get:

### Fixed buttons (always in the HTML)

- **EE −X** — calls `GET /api/jog_ee?dx=-0.35`
- **EE +X** — calls `GET /api/jog_ee?dx=0.35`

### Dynamic buttons (rebuilt every ~250 ms from `/api/state`)

For **each joint** reported in `j.joints`, the script adds:

- **`J<n> −`** — `GET /api/jog_joint?j=<n>&delta=-0.5`
- **`J<n> +`** — `GET /api/jog_joint?j=<n>&delta=0.5`

So joint jog steps are **±0.5°** per click.

### Not buttons

- The **`sw`** row is **limit indicators** (colored dots + “Jn limit” text), not clickable controls.
- The **SVG** is only a drawing; there’s no click-to-move on it.

```503:526:c:\Users\maxwe\OneDrive\Documents\GitHub\Robot Arm Sim\Robot-Arm-Sim\firmware\pico_control_script.py

...
document.getElementById('em').onclick=function(){fetch('/api/jog_ee?dx=-0.35');};
document.getElementById('ep').onclick=function(){fetch('/api/jog_ee?dx=0.35');};
```

ENTRY 400  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what changed:

### Dashboard JS (`pico_control_script.py` embedded HTML)

1. **Hold-to-move** — Uses **`pointerdown`** → immediate jog **`fetch`**, then **`setInterval` every 65 ms** while pressed. **`pointerup` / `pointercancel` / `lostpointercapture`** plus **`window` `pointerup`** and **`blur`** stop motion so releasing anywhere ends the hold.

2. **Pointer capture** — `setPointerCapture` on **`pointerdown`** so dragging slightly off the control doesn’t lose the press.

3. **Touch / UX** — `touch-action: manipulation`, **`user-select: none`** on buttons (less lag / accidental selection).

4. **Live UI without nuking controls** — **`/api/state` ~every 66 ms (~15 Hz)** updates:
   - EE text  
   - SVG arm path  
   - Limit dots **in place** (rebuild only when the joint index list changes)  
   - Joint buttons **only when** the joint index signature changes (not every poll)

So held buttons keep their listeners; polling no longer replaces `#jb`/`#sw` HTML every tick.

### Firmware safety

- After each **`/api/jog_joint`** / **`/api/jog_ee`** enqueue, **`_web_cmd_queue` is trimmed** so length stays ≤ **64**, avoiding unbounded growth if HTTP outruns the main loop.

Redeploy **`main.py`** to the Pico (Hardware → Deploy) so the new page is served.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 405  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what changed to make the dashboard feel **more real-time**:

### Faster, smoother telemetry loop
- Replaced fixed **`setInterval(..., 66)`** with an **`async pollLoop`**: each **`/api/state`** finishes, then waits **14 ms** before the next fetch (~**70 Hz** when the Pico/Wi‑Fi keeps up; if a request is slow, spacing grows automatically).
- **`fetch(..., { cache: 'no-store' })`** so the browser doesn’t serve a cached `/api/state`.

### Tighter motion while holding
- Hold repeat interval **65 ms → 28 ms** so jog commands stream faster.

### Pose updates right after a jog
- Every **`fireHold()`** `fetch` **`.then(() => refreshState())`** so EE text / arm SVG refresh **as soon as the jog returns**, instead of waiting for the next poll tick.

### No overlapping `/api/state` storms
- **`stateBusy` + `statePending`**: only one state fetch at a time; if something asks during a fetch (e.g. poll + jog), **one follow-up** runs right after.

Redeploy firmware so the Pico serves the new page. If Wi‑Fi starts lagging, bump **14** up slightly (e.g. **25**) or **28** up a bit—the loop self-limits when RTT is high.

[REDACTED]

<script>
function armLine(points){if(points.length<2)return '';
let mx=0,my=0,Mx=0,My=0;for(const p of points){mx=Math.min(mx,p[0]);Mx=Math.max(Mx,p[0]);my=Math.min(my,p[1]);My=Math.max(My,p[1]);}
const rw=Math.max(Mx-mx,1e-6),rh=Math.max(My-my,1e-6),sc=Math.min(180/rw,180/rh)*0.85,cx=(mx+Mx)/2,cy=(my+My)/2;
let d='';for(let i=0;i<points.length;i++){const x=(points[i][0]-cx)*sc,y=-(points[i][1]-cy)*sc;d+=(i?'L':'M')+x+','+y;}
return d;}
var jbBuiltSig='',swBuiltSig='',holdIv=null,holdUrl=null;
var stateBusy=false,statePending=false;
function rebuildSwitches(swArr){var el=document.getElementById('sw'),h='';for(var i=0;i<swArr.length;i++){h+='<span><span class="dot na"></span>J'+swArr[i].idx+' limit</span> ';}el.innerHTML=h.trimEnd();}
function updateSwitchDots(swArr){var el=document.getElementById('sw');for(var i=0;i<swArr.length;i++){var span=el.children[i];if(!span)return false;var dot=span.querySelector('.dot');if(!dot)return false;var s=swArr[i];dot.className='dot '+(s.pressed?'on':(s.wired?'off':'na'));}return true;}
function rebuildJointButtons(joints){var el=document.getElementById('jb');el.innerHTML='';for(var i=0;i<joints.length;i++){var jo=joints[i];var bm=document.createElement('button');bm.type='button';bm.textContent='J'+jo.idx+' −';bindHold(bm,'/api/jog_joint?j='+jo.idx+'&delta=-0.5');el.appendChild(bm);var bp=document.createElement('button');bp.type='button';bp.textContent='J'+jo.idx+' +';bindHold(bp,'/api/jog_joint?j='+jo.idx+'&delta=0.5');el.appendChild(bp);el.appendChild(document.createTextNode(' '));}}
async function refreshState(){
if(stateBusy){statePending=true;return;}
stateBusy=true;
try{
var r=await fetch('/api/state',{cache:'no-store'});
var j=await r.json();
document.getElementById('ee').textContent='EE X='+j.ee.x+' Y='+j.ee.y+' Z='+j.ee.z+(j.fk_ok?'':' (FK off)');
var swSig=j.switches.map(function(s){return s.idx;}).join('|');
if(swSig!==swBuiltSig){rebuildSwitches(j.switches);swBuiltSig=swSig;}else if(!updateSwitchDots(j.switches)){rebuildSwitches(j.switches);}
var jbSig=j.joints.map(function(x){return x.idx;}).join('|');
if(jbSig!==jbBuiltSig){rebuildJointButtons(j.joints);jbBuiltSig=jbSig;}
var pts=j.polyline_xy||[];document.getElementById('armPath').setAttribute('d',pts.length?armLine(pts):'');
}catch(e){console.warn(e);}
finally{
stateBusy=false;
if(statePending){statePending=false;refreshState();}
}
}
async function pollLoop(){
while(true){
try{await refreshState();await new Promise(function(r){setTimeout(r,14);});}
catch(e){console.warn(e);await new Promise(function(r){setTimeout(r,80);});}
}
}
pollLoop();
function stopHold(){if(holdIv){clearInterval(holdIv);holdIv=null;}holdUrl=null;}
function fireHold(){
if(!holdUrl)return;
fetch(holdUrl,{cache:'no-store',keepalive:true}).then(function(){refreshState();}).catch(function(){});
}
function startHold(url){stopHold();holdUrl=url;fireHold();holdIv=setInterval(fireHold,28);}
function bindHold(el,url){
el.addEventListener('pointerdown',function(e){if(e.pointerType==='mouse'&&e.button!==0)return;e.preventDefault();try{el.setPointerCapture(e.pointerId);}catch(x){}startHold(url);});
el.addEventListener('pointerup',stopHold);el.addEventListener('pointercancel',stopHold);el.addEventListener('lostpointercapture',stopHold);}
bindHold(document.getElementById('em'),'/api/jog_ee?dx=-0.35');
bindHold(document.getElementById('ep'),'/api/jog_ee?dx=0.35');
window.addEventListener('blur',stopHold);
window.addEventListener('pointerup',stopHold);
</script>
</body></html>

ENTRY 414  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was implemented:

### Firmware
- **`JE` commands** now carry **`dx`, `dy`, `dz`** (world-space mm). Older **`("JE", dx)`** tuples still work (`dy`/`dz` default to `0`).
- **`GET /api/jog_ee`** accepts optional **`dx`**, **`dy`**, **`dz`** (default `"0"`). Examples: `?dx=0.35`, `?dy=-0.35`, `?dz=0.35`. IK runs at **`ee + (dx,dy,dz)`**.

### Dashboard UI
- **EE row:** six buttons — **X − / X +**, **Y − / Y +**, **Z − / Z +** (same **0.35 mm** step and hold-to-repeat behavior as before).
- **Joint row:** unchanged **`J<n> −` / `J<n> +`** (±**0.5°**), with headings **“End-effector — world X/Y/Z (mm)”** and **“Joints — degrees”** so cartesian vs joint jog is obvious.

Redeploy **`main.py`** to the Pico so the new page and `/api/jog_ee` behavior are live.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 424  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s what was wrong and what changed:

### Bug fix
In `refreshState`, **`gy` was wired to `#gz`**, so the Y field never tracked correctly. That’s fixed (`gx` / `gy` / `gz`), and while none of those inputs are focused they’re **synced from live `/api/state`** so they stay aligned with the arm.

### EE controls (restored + clearer)
- **Six jog buttons** again use explicit labels: **EE −X / EE +X**, **EE −Y / EE +Y**, **EE −Z / EE +Z** (still **0.35 mm** per tick, hold to slew).

### Move anywhere in workspace (IK permitting)
- **X / Y / Z number fields + “Go”** call new **`GET /api/goto_ee?x=&y=&z=`** with absolute **world mm**.
- Firmware handles **`("JG", tx, ty, tz)`** by running **`_ik_try_world(tx, ty, tz)`** and applying the solution (same as incremental EE jog). If the pose isn’t reachable, IK returns nothing and there’s no motion (same as before for failed IK).

Redeploy **`main.py`** to the Pico so the updated page and **`/api/goto_ee`** route are active.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 433  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
No — **joint jog is still there.**

In `pico_control_script.py` the page still has:

- A **“Joint jog — degrees (hold)”** line  
- An empty row **`id="jb"`** that gets filled by **`rebuildJointButtons()`** on each `/api/state` poll  
- One **`J<n> −`** and **`J<n> +`** per joint (±**0.5°**, hold-to-repeat), calling **`/api/jog_joint`**

Only the **coordinate “Go to EE”** block was removed; EE XYZ buttons and joint buttons were kept.

If you **don’t see** any joint buttons in the browser, it’s usually because **`j.joints`** from `/api/state` is empty (e.g. axes not loaded / FK issue). The EE row would still show; say if that matches what you see and we can trace `_http_axes_ref` / axis loading next.

ENTRY 437  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
## Does the firmware use PIO for step pulses?

**Sometimes.** It depends on the joint driver:

| Driver | STEP generation |
|--------|------------------|
| **`28byj`** | **No PIO.** Four GPIO outputs drive the half‑step coil pattern in Python (`StepperAxis28BYJ._step`). |
| **`stepdir`** + **`pio_stepdir: true`** (default) | **Yes.** STEP comes from a **PIO state machine** (`StepperAxisStepDirPIO`). |
| **`stepdir`** + **`pio_stepdir: false`** | **No.** STEP is **bit‑banged** on GPIO with `time.sleep_us(3)` around each edge (`StepperAxisStepDir`). |

PIO is only compiled in if `rp2` / `@asm_pio` works; otherwise step/dir must use GPIO STEP (see comments around `_PIO_HAVE_STEP`).

The PIO program pulls a **delay word** from the TX FIFO, waits in a tight delay loop, then does a short **STEP high → STEP low** on the **PIO `set` pins** (STEP pin only). So each physical pulse is timed by the PIO clock, not by Python sleeps.

## How CPU work relates to step pulses

Rough pipeline:

1. **Main loop** (driven by **`STEP_US`**) runs serial/WiFi, homing, web queue, etc., then updates motion and calls into each axis’s stepper logic.
2. **Planning** (velocity / accel / jerk, caps like **`MAX_STEPS_PER_AXIS_TICK`**) decides **how many steps** and direction **this iteration**.
3. **`_step(count, forward)`** turns that into hardware:
   - **DIR** is always handled on the **CPU**: set the DIR pin, optional **`dir_setup_us`** delay so drivers see stable direction before edges.
   - **STEP**:
     - **GPIO path:** CPU toggles STEP and **`sleep_us`** — Python **blocks** for the whole pulse train.
     - **PIO path:** CPU **`put`s one FIFO word per step** (a computed **`period`** tied to clock frequency / velocity estimate). PIO emits that pulse **while the CPU can move on** to other things (subject to FIFO blocking if you enqueue faster than PIO drains).

So: **motion decisions and DIR setup are CPU‑centric; STEP timing for NEMA‑style drivers is meant to be PIO‑offloaded when enabled**, which avoids tying the interpreter to microsecond‑scale toggles for every edge.

**Tradeoffs:** PIO improves **pulse regularity** and reduces **CPU time inside `_step`**, but you still only **plan** at the main loop rate (**`STEP_US`**), and you consume **one state machine per step/dir axis** (up to 8). GPIO STEP is simpler and fine for bring‑up but jitters more under load.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 442  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s how **`pico_control_script.py`** treats concurrency:

## What actually runs in parallel

MicroPython’s **`_thread`** is used **only when Wi‑Fi is enabled** (`ENABLE_WIFI` and a SSID). Then **two extra threads** are started after STA comes up:

1. **`_wifi_tcp_loop`** — LAN **TCP command server** (`accept`, `recv`, line parsing, optional upload). Incoming command lines are **`append`ed to `_wifi_rx`**; replies are queued by having the main loop pass **`_wifi_tx.append`** as the reply sink, and this thread **`pop`s `_wifi_tx`** and **`sendall`s** on the socket.

2. **`_web_http_server_thread`** — **HTTP dashboard** (`accept`, parse request, build response). Jog/state handlers **`append` to `_web_cmd_queue`**.

Everything else — **USB serial polling**, **motion / stepping**, **homing**, **draining those queues**, **`STEP_US` timing** — runs in the **`main()` loop on the main thread**.

## Deliberately *not* threaded: Wi‑Fi association

**`wlan.connect` / DHCP** run in **`_wifi_sta_connect_blocking` on the main thread**, with comments that on Pico W / CYW43 doing association only inside a **new** thread **often never completes**. Only after that succeeds do the TCP and HTTP **worker** threads start.

## How “multi-threading” is reconciled with motors

The design is **single writer to motion hardware**:

- Background threads **never call `axes.update()` / `_step()` directly**.
- They only push **text commands** (`_wifi_rx`) or **structured web ops** (`_web_cmd_queue`).
- Each **`STEP_US` iteration**, the main loop **drains `_wifi_rx`** and feeds lines through the same **`_handle_line`** path as USB, then **drains `_web_cmd_queue`** and applies **`_apply_web_command`**.

So threads are mostly **I/O and parsing**; **planning and pulses stay serial** on the main loop.

## Synchronization model (important caveat)

`_wifi_rx`, `_wifi_tx`, and `_web_cmd_queue` are **plain Python lists** shared between threads. The comment calls web append “thread-safe” in spirit (producer on HTTP thread, consumer on main), but there are **no explicit locks** in this file. On MicroPython’s **`_thread`**, cross-thread list **`append` / `pop(0)`** is the usual pattern and often works in practice, but it’s **not formally mutex-protected** here — that’s a tradeoff (simplicity vs. theoretical races under heavy load).

## Summary

| Piece | Thread |
|--------|--------|
| Wi‑Fi join / DHCP | **Main** |
| Main motor + USB loop | **Main** |
| LAN TCP server | **Worker** → queues lines |
| HTTP server | **Worker** → queues jog/state ops |
| Actual stepping / DIR / PIO | **Main only** |

So “multi-threading” here means **network I/O separated onto two helpers**, with **command forwarding through lists** into the **single real-time control loop** — not parallel motion planners or parallel GPIO from multiple threads.

ENTRY 445  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Here’s a practical picture for **normal Raspberry Pi Pico MicroPython firmware** (the `.uf2` you get from micropython.org / Raspberry Pi — same VM your arm script runs on).

## Two cores exist; Python doesn’t automatically fill both

The RP2040 (and the ARM side of RP2350 on Pico 2) has **two cores**. MicroPython still starts your program as **one main interpreter thread**, which runs on **core 0**.

If you **never** use **`_thread.start_new_thread(...)`**, then **your Python code is effectively single-core**: almost all bytecode execution for your script happens on **core 0**. Core 1 is usually **idle** from Python’s point of view (it might still be touched by very low-level ROM/SDK bits, but not running your second Python task).

## When both cores get used from Python

MicroPython’s **`_thread`** module is how **second-core execution** is exposed:

- **`start_new_thread`** runs the given function in a **separate Python thread**.
- On the **rp2** port, those worker threads are **scheduled on the second core** while the **main thread stays on core 0** (that’s the usual Pico MicroPython design for `_thread`, so Wi‑Fi/TCP-style workers don’t have to time-slice on core 0 only).

So:

| Situation | Typical core use |
|-----------|-------------------|
| REPL / script, **no** `_thread` | **Core 0** for your Python; core 1 idle |
| Script uses **`_thread`** (e.g. network loops) | **Core 0** main loop + **core 1** worker thread(s) |

Your **robot firmware** fits the second row **when Wi‑Fi is enabled**: it starts the **TCP** and **HTTP** loops with **`_thread.start_new_thread`**, so **both cores can be busy** (network threads vs main motion loop). With **Wi‑Fi off**, it never starts those threads → **back to effectively one core for Python**.

## Not the same as “SMP everywhere”

This is **not** automatic parallel speedup for all code: only **explicit threads** (and whatever the port does underneath, e.g. CYW43) use core 1. There’s no magic splitting of one Python loop across both cores.

## If you meant bare-metal / C SDK instead

In **C/C++ Pico SDK** firmware you’d use something like **`multicore_launch_core1()`** to run a second core routine — that’s orthogonal to MicroPython; **stock MicroPython doesn’t give you that API** from Python except via **`_thread`**.

**Short answer:** **Regular MicroPython firmware uses core 0 for the main program by default.** **Both cores are used only when you spawn `_thread` workers** (your Wi‑Fi firmware does that); otherwise it’s effectively **single-core for your script**.

<user_query>
how could you enable dual threading to speed up performance for regular firmware
</user_query>

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
ENTRY 447  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
You don’t “flip on dual threading” in MicroPython like a BIOS option. The chip already has two cores; **you speed things up only by moving specific work to a second `_thread` worker** while keeping **motion + stepping + tight timing on the main thread**.

## What actually buys performance

**Good candidates for a second thread (core 1 in typical Pico MicroPython builds)**  
Work that is **CPU-heavy but not timing-critical** and talks to the motion loop through **queues/messages**:

- **Network I/O** you already pattern-match: accept/read HTTP or TCP, parse text, **enqueue commands** (your `_wifi_rx` / `_web_cmd_queue` style).
- **Chunky parsing**: long JSON, big string processing, base64 decode paths — *after* you’ve received bytes in the worker.
- **Logging / formatting** that would otherwise block the main loop (still use a bounded queue so RAM doesn’t explode).
- **Non-real-time housekeeping**: periodic stats, building large responses before send.

**Bad candidates (usually stay on main thread)**  
Anything that must stay **deterministic** or **directly touch motors**:

- The **`STEP_US` loop**: planning, `axis.update()`, `_step()`, PIO `put`, homing state machine.
- **Shared motor state** without locks (easy to corrupt `current_pos` / targets).
- **Tight coordination with USB** where ordering matters unless you formalize a queue protocol.

Parallelizing “half the motion planner on core 0 and half on core 1” is almost never worth it in MicroPython: **two interpreters sharing axes without a rigorous design** is slower *and* risky.

## How you’d “enable” it in practice

1. **`import _thread`** and **`start_new_thread(worker, ())`** for a clear worker entrypoint.
2. **`_thread.allocate_lock()`** (or queues only touched with one writer / one reader pattern) for **any** shared globals the worker and main loop both touch.
3. **Bounded queues** (max length + drop or merge policy) between worker and main — same idea as your web command cap.
4. **Main loop stays authoritative**: worker **never** calls `_step()`; it only **submits** intents.

That’s the same architectural move as your Wi‑Fi threads: **I/O and parsing off-core, motion on-core**.

## Where threading often *doesn’t* speed things up

- **Motor stepping** is usually limited by **mechanics / driver / loop rate**, not “missing a second core.”
- **Garbage collection** still affects the whole VM; another thread can trigger allocations that indirectly stress core 0.
- **CYW43 Wi‑Fi** already consumes time and sometimes interacts with system layers; more threads ≠ free bandwidth.

## If you meant “regular firmware” as **C SDK**, not MicroPython

Then you’d use **`multicore_launch_core1()`** / FIFOs between cores, split **blocking I/O vs control**, and use **mutexes** around shared hardware — same separation principle, different API.

---

**Bottom line:** enable dual-thread speedups by **identifying non-real-time bottlenecks**, moving them to **`_thread` workers**, and keeping a **single-threaded motion core path** fed by **queues** — not by trying to parallelize the step loop itself.

[REDACTED]

ENTRY 448  ROLE: user
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
<user_query>
how could it speed up the performance for our firmware
</user_query>

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
ENTRY 449  ROLE: assistant
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
For **your** setup (`pico_control_script.py` + MicroPython on Pico W / 2W), threading speeds things up only where **network / parsing / I/O** would otherwise steal time from the **`STEP_US` motion loop**. It does **not** make stepping itself “2× faster.”

## What you already gain from threading

With **`ENABLE_WIFI` + SSID**, you already start:

- **`_wifi_tcp_loop`** — TCP accept/read/upload  
- **`_web_http_server_thread`** — HTTP accept/parse/respond  

Those run as **`_thread` workers**, so **blocking `accept()` / `recv()` / building HTTP bodies** mostly happens **off the core that runs the main motion loop**. That directly **reduces jitter** and **missed loop deadlines** versus doing all of that inline on the same thread as `axes.update()` / `_step()`.

So for **Wi‑Fi builds**, dual-threading is already doing the main useful job: **keep I/O off the real-time path.**

## Where more threading *could* help *your* firmware (marginal / situational)

These are realistic extensions of the same pattern (worker produces **messages**, main loop **consumes**):

1. **USB path paranoia**  
   Today the main loop **`poll`s stdin one character at a time** interleaved with motion. If profiling showed USB/host chatter starving the loop, you could explore **buffering lines on another thread** (UART/raw reads → queue of lines). That’s **port-dependent** and easy to get wrong; often **fixing protocol burst size** on the host helps more.

2. **Heavy command handling off the hot path**  
   Anything that does **lots of Python work per line** (big parses, debug dumps) could **enqueue** work and return quickly, with the worker finishing formatting/logging. Only worth it if you’ve measured **multi‑millisecond** stalls in `_handle_line`.

3. **Telemetry formatting**  
   **`POS:` / `PINS:`** string building every second could move to a worker that pushes **preformatted strings** into a one-slot mailbox. Small win unless those paths grow.

## Where threading **won’t** materially speed up **your** firmware

- **Step generation**: **`STEP_US`**, **`axis_plan_velocity_steps`**, **`_step()`**, **PIO `put`** — should stay on **one** coherent loop; splitting across cores adds locking and usually **hurts** latency.
- **IK inside `_apply_web_command` / jog**: cheap compared to Wi‑Fi; threading rarely wins.
- **“More cores = higher step rate”**: ceiling is **driver/motor/mechanics + `max_sps` + loop overrun**, not CPU core count.

## Often beats adding threads

- **Fewer allocations** in the motion loop (smaller GC pauses on core 0).  
- **Lower USB/Wi‑Fi traffic** when sync is on (host-side rate cap).  
- **Raise `STEP_US`** if the loop overruns (comments in firmware already say this).  
- **Keep Wi‑Fi HTTP polling sane** (browser poll rate) so the HTTP worker isn’t constantly hammered.

**Bottom line for your firmware:** threading mainly speeds things up by **keeping LAN servers off the motion core**, which you already do when Wi‑Fi is on. Further gains come from **moving rare heavy work behind queues** or **reducing load/GC on the main loop**, not from parallelizing stepping or IK across cores.
